(45; original 303): Hist. of (ancient) Christianity, Am. ed., p. 432.

(46; original 304): Chateaubriand commends the monastic institution mainly under the first view. "If there are refuges for the health of the body, ah ! permit religion to have such also for the health of the soul, which is still more subject to sickness, and the infirmities of which are so much more sad, so much more tedious and difficult to cure!" Montalembert (l.c. i. 25) objects to this view as poetic and touching but false, and represents monasticism as an arena for the healthiest and strongest souls which the world has ever produced, and quotes the passage of Chrysostom: "Come and see the tents of the soldiers of Christ; come and see their order of battle; they fight every day, and every day they defeat and immolate the passions which assail us."

(47; original 305): 1 The abbot Isidore of Pelusium wrote to a slaveholder, Ep. l. i. 142 (cited by Neander): "I did not think that the man who loves Christ, and knows the grace which makes us all free, would still hold slaves."

(48; original 306): The monkish miracles, with which the Vitae Patrum of the Jesuit Rosweyde and the Acta Sanctorum swarm, often contradict all the laws of nature and of reason, and would be hardly worthy of mention, but that they come from such fathers as Jerome, Rufinus, Severus, Palladius, and Theodoret, and go to characterize the Nicene age. We are far from rejecting all and every one as falsehood and deception, and accepting the judgment of Isaac Taylor (Ancient Christianity, ii. 106): "The Nicene miracles are of a kind which shocks every sentiment of gravity, of decency, and of piety:—in their obvious features they are childish, horrid, blasphemous, and foul." Much more cautious is the opinion of Robertson (Hist. of the Christian Church, i. 312) and other Protestant historians, who suppose that, together with the innocent illusions of a heated imagination and the fabrications of intentional fraud, there must have been also much that was real, though in the nature of the case an exact sifting is impossible. But many of these stories are too much even for Roman credulity, and are either entirely omitted or at least greatly reduced and modified by critical historians. We read not only of innumerable visions, prophecies, healings of the sick and the possessed, but also of raising of the dead (as in the life of Martin of Tours), of the growth of a dry stick into a fruitful tree, and of a monk’s passing unseared, in absolute obedience to his abbot, through a furnace of fire as through a cooling bath. (Comp. Sulp. Sever. Dial. i. c. 12 and 13.) Even wild beasts play a large part, and are transformed into rational servants of the Egyptian saints of the desert. At the funeral of Paul of Thebes, according to Jerome, two lions voluntarily performed the office of sexton. Pachomius walked unharmed over serpents and scorpions, and crossed the Nile on crocodiles, which, of their own accord, presented their backs. The younger Macarius, or (according to other statements of the Historia Lausiaca; comp. the investigation of Tillemont, tom. viii. p. 811 sqq.) the monk Marcus stood on so good terms with the beasts, that a hyena (according to Rufinus, V. P. ii. 4, it was a lioness) brought her young one to him in his cell, that he might open its eyes; which he did by prayer and application of spittle; and the next day she offered him, for gratitude, a large sheepskin; the saint at first declined the gift, and reproved the beast for the double crime of murder and theft, by which she had obtained the skin; but when the hyena showed repentance, and with a nod promised amendment, Macarius took the skin, and afterward bequeathed it to the great bishop Athanasius. Severus (Dial. i. c. 9) gives a very similar account of an unknown anchoret, but, like Rufinus, substitutes for the hyena of Palladius a lioness with five whelps, and makes the saint receive the present of the skin without scruple or reproof. Shortly before (c. 8), he speaks, however, of a wolf, which once robbed a friendly hermit, whose evening meal she was accustomed to share, showed deep repentance for it, and with bowed head begged forgiveness of the saint. Perhaps Palladius or his Latin translator has combined these two anecdotes.

(49; original 307): Pliny counts thirty-nine different sorts of palm trees, of which the best grow in Egypt, are ever green, have thick foliage, and bear a fruit, from which in some places bread is made.

(50; original 308): Jerome says of Anthony, in his Vita Pauli Theb. (c. i.): "Non tam ipse auto omnes (eremitas) fuit, quam ab eo omnium incitata sunt studia."

(51; original 309): According to the common opinion, which was also Augustine’s, Anthony could not even read. But Tillemont (tom. vii. 107 and 666), Butler, and others think that this igorance related only to the Greek alphabet, not to the Egyptian. Athanasius, p. 795, expresses himself somewhat indistinctly; that, from dread of society, he would not maqei'n gravmmata (letters? or the arts?), but speaks afterward of his regard for reading.

(52; original 310): Augustine says of him, De doctr. Christ. § 4, that, without being able to read from only hearing the Bible, he knew it by heart. The life of Athanasius shows, indeed, that a number of Scripture passages were very familiar to him. But of a connected and deep knowledge of Scripture in him, or in these anchorets generally, we find no trace.

(53; original 311): Matt. xix. 21.

(54; original 312): Matt. vi. 34.

(55; original 313): Eij" parqenw'na, says Athanasius; i.e., not "un monastere de verges," as Tillemont translates, for nunneries did not yet exist; but a society of female ascetics within the congregation; from which, however, a regular cloister might of course very easily grow.

(56; original 314): Jerome, in his Vita Hilarionis, c. 31, gives an incidental description of this last residence of Anthony, according to which it was not so desolate as from Athanasius one would infer. He speaks even of palms, fruit trees, and vines in this garden, the fruit of which any one would have enjoyed.

(57; original 315): Athanas. c. 42: jElqovnte" ga;r (oiJ ejcqroi;)oJpoivou" ajn eu{rwsin hJma'",toiou'toi kai; aujtoi; givnontai, etc.—an important psychological observation.

(58; original 316): This is not told indeed by Athanasius, but by Rufinus, Jerome, and Socrates (Hist. Eccl. iv. 25). Comp. Tillemont, l.c. p. 129.

(59; original 317): Comp. the proofs in Tillemont, l.c. p. 137 sq.

(60; original 318): Dion, fol. 51, ed. Petav., cited in Tillemont and Neander.

(61; original 319): Hom. viii. in Matth. tom. vii. 128 (ed. Montfaucon).

(62; original 320): · Comp. Aug.: Confess. l. viii. c. 6 and 28.

(63; original 321): "Quanti populi," says Rufinus (Vitae Patr. ii c. 7), "habentur in urbibus, tantae paene habentur in desertis multitudines monachorum." Gibbon adds the sarcastic remark: "Posterity might repeat the saying, which had formerly been applied to sacred animals of the same country, That in Egypt it was less difficult to find a god than a man." Montalembert (Monks of the West, vol. i. p. 314) says of the increase of monks: "Nothing in the wonderful history of these hermits in Egypt is so incredible as their number. But the most weighty authorities agreed in establishing it (S. Augustine, De morib. Eccles. i. 31). It was a kind of emigration of towns to the desert, of civilization to simplicity, of noise to silence, of corruption to innocence. The current once begun, floods of men, of women, and of children threw themselves into it, and flowed thither during a century with irresistible force."

(64; original 322): There were several (five or seven) anchorets of this name, who are often confounded. The most celebrated are Macarius the elder, or the Great († 390), to whom the Homilies probably belong; and Macarius the younger, of Alexandria († 404), the teacher of Palladius, who spent a long time with him, and set him as high as the other. Comp. Tillemont’s extended account, tom. viii. p. 574-650, and the notes, p. 811 sqq.

(65; original 323): On Ammon, or, in Egyptian, Amus and Amun, comp. Tillemont, viii. p. 153-166, and the notes, p. 672-674.

(66; original 324): Opera, tom. ii. p. 13-40.

(67; original 325): Lu. xiv. 33.

(68; original 326): A peculiar, romantic, but not fully historical interest attaches to the biography of the imprisoned and fortunately escaping monk Malchus, with his nominal wife, which is preserved to us by Jerome.

(69; original 327): Original at least in the Christian church. Gieseler refers to a heathen precedent; the Fallobatei'" in Syria, mentioned by Lucian, De Dea Syria, c. 28 and 29.

(70; original 328): Butler, l.c., however, relates something similar of a contemporary Benedictine monk, Dom Claude Leante: "In 1731, when he was about fifty-one years of age, he had fasted eleven years without taking any food the whole forty days, except what he daily took at mass; and what added to the wonder is, that during Lent he did not properly sleep, but only dozed. He could not bear the open air; and toward the end of Lent he was excessively pale and wasted. This fact is attested by his brethren and superiors, in a relation printed at Sens, in 1731."

(71; original 329): The first pillar, which he himself erected, and on which he lived four years, was six cubits (phvcewn) high, the second twelve, the third twenty-two, and the fourth, which the people erected for him, and on which he spent twenty years, was thirty-six, according to Theodoret; others say forty. The top was only three feet in diameter. It probably had a railing, however, on which he could lean in sleep or exhaustion. So at least these pillars are drawn in pictures. Food was carried up to the pillar saints by their disciples on a ladder.

(72; original 330): To; mevga qau'ma th'" oijkoumevnh". Hist. Relig. c. 26, at the beginning.

(73; original 331): Referring to Isa xx. 2; Jer. i. 17; xxviii. 12; Hos i. 2; iii. 1; Ezek. iv. 4; xii. 5.

(74; original 332): Ep. ii. 114; cited in Gieseler, ii. 2, p. 246, note 47 (Edinb. Engl. ed. ii. p. 13, note 47), and in Neander.

(75; original 333): Möhler remarks on this (Vermischte Schriften, ii. p. 183): "Thus antiquity expresses its faith, that for man perfectly reconciled with God there is no enemy in nature. There is more than poetry here; there is expressed at least the high opinion his own and future generations had of Pachomius." The last qualifying remark suggests a doubt even in the mind of this famous modern champion of Romanism as to the real historical character of the wonderful tales of this monastic saint.

(76; original 334): Gregory Nazianzen, in his eulogy on Basil (Orat. xx. of the old order, Orat. xliii. in the new Par. ed.), gives him the honor of endeavoring to unite the theoretical and the practical modes of life in monasticism, i]na mhvte to; filovsofon ajkoinwvnhton h|/, mhvte to; praktiko;n ajfilovsofon.

(77; original 335): Comp. Neander, iii. 487 (Torrey’s translation, vol. ii. p. 250 sqq.), who esteems Nilus highly; and the article of Gass in Herzog’s Theol. Encykl. vol. x. p, 355 sqq. His works are in the Bibl. Max. vet. Patr. tom. vii., and in Migne’s Patrol. Gr. t. 79.

(78; original 336): Comp. on him Tillemont, xv., and H. A. Niemeyer: "De Isid. Pel. vita, scripet doctrina," Hal. 1825. His Epistles are in the 7th volume of the Bibliotheca Maxima, and in Migne’s Patrol. Graeca, tom. 58, Paris, 1860.

(79; original 337): From @ylix]lim' = Eujcivtai, from eujchv, prayer.

(80; original 338): Sulpitius Severus, in the first of his three dialogues, gives several amusing instances of the difference between the Gallic and Egyptian stomach, and was greatly astonished when the first Egyptian anchoret whom he visited placed before him and his four companions a half loaf of barley bread and a handful of herbs for a dinner, though they tasted very good after the wearisome journey. "Edacitas," says he, "in Graecis gula est, in Gallia natura." (Dial. i. c. 8, in Gallandi, t. viii. p. 405.)

(81; original 339): "The monastic stream," says Montalembert, l.c., "which had been born in the deserts of Egypt, divided itself into two great arms. The one spread in the East, at first inundated everything, then concentrated and lost itself there. The other escaped into the West, and spread itself by a thousand channels over an entire world, which had to be covered and fertilized."

(82; original 340): Augustine, Conf. vii. 6: "Erat monasterium Mediolani plenum bonis fratribus extra urbis moenia, sub Ambrosio nutritore."

(83; original 341): Ambr.: De virginibus, lib. iii., addressed to his sister Marcellina, about 377. Comp. Tillem. x. 102-105, and Schröckh, viii. 355 sqq.

(84; original 342): Ambr.: Hexaëmeron, l. iii. c. 5. Hieron.: Ep. ad Oceanum de morte Fabiolae, Ep. 77 ed. Vall. (84 ed. Ben., al. 30).

(85; original 343): He himself speaks of a monasterium clericorum in his episcopal residence, and his biographer, Possidius, says of him, Vita, c. 5: "Factus ergo presbyter monasterium inter ecclesiam mox instituit, et cum Dei servis vivere coepit secundum modum, et regulam sub sanctis apostlis constitutam, maxime ut nemo quidquam proprium haberet, sed eis essent omnia communia."

(86; original 344): De opera monach. c. 22. Still later, Salvian (De gubern. Dei, viii. 4) speaks of the hatred of the Africans for monasticism.

(87; original 345): In his Vita Martini, and also in three letters respecting him, and in three very eloquently and elegantly written dialogues, the first of which relates to the oriental monks, the two others to the miracles of Martin (translated, with some omissions, in Ruffner’s Fathers of the Desert, vol. ii. p. 68-178). He tells us (Dial. i. c. 23) that the book traders of Rome sold his Vita Martini more rapidly than any other book, and made great profit on it. The Acts of the Saints were read as romances in those days.

(88; original 346): The biographer here refers, of course, to Matt. xxv. 40

(89; original 347): Toward the close of his biography, c. 26, 27 (Gallandi, tom. viii. 399).

(90; original 348): Comp. Dial. ii. 5 (in Gallandi Bibl. tom. viii. p. 412).

(91; original 349): Vita M. c. 20 (in Gallandi, viii. 397).

(92; original 350): Dial. ii. 7, which probably relates to the same banquet, since Martin declined other invitations to the imperial table. Severus gives us to understand that this was the only time Martin allowed a woman so near him, or received her service. He commended a nun for declining even his official visit as bishop, and Severus remarks thereupon: "O glorious virgin, who would not even suffer herself to be seen by Martin! O blessed Martin, who took not this refusal for an insult, but commended its virtue, and rejoiced to find in that region so rare an example!" (Dial, ii. c. 12, Gall, viii. 414.)

(93; original 351): As he himself boasts in his second apology to Rufinus: "Ego philosophus(?), rhetor, grammaticus, dialecticus, hebraeus, graecus, latinus, trilinguis." The celebrated Erasmus, the first editor of his works, and a very competent judge in matters of literary talent and merit, places Jerome above all the fathers, even St. Augustine (with whose doctrines of free grace and predestination he could not sympathize), and often gives eloquent expression to his admiration for him. In a letter to Pope Leo X. (Ep. ii. 1, quoted in Vallarsi’s ed. of Jerome’s works, tom. xi. 290), he says: "Divus Hieronymus sic apud Latinos est theologorum princeps, ut hunc prope solum habeamus theologi dignum nomine. Non quod caeteros damnem, sed quod illustres alioqui, si cum hoc conferantur, ob huius eminentiam velut obscurentur. Denique tot egregiis est cumulatus dotibus, ut vix ullum habeat et ipsa docta Graecia, quem cum hoc viro quest componere. Quantum in illo Romanae facundiae! quanta linguarum peritia! quanta omnis antiquitatis omnium historiarum notitia! quam fida memoria! quam felix rerum omnium mixtum! quam absoluta mysticarum literarum cognitio! super omnia, quis ardor ille, quam admirabilis divini pectoris afflatus? ut una et plurimum delectet eloquentia, et doceat eruditione, et rapiat sanctimonia."

(94; original 352): Hence called Stridonensis; also in distinction from the contemporary but little known Greek Jerome, who was probably a presbyter in Jerusalem.

(95; original 353): Martianay, Stilting, Cave, Schröckh, Hagenbach, and others, place his birth, according to Prosper, Chron. ad ann. 331, in the year 331; Baronius, Du Pin, and Tillemont, with greater probability, in the year 342. The last infers from various circumstances, that Jerome lived, not ninety-one years, as Prosper states, but only seventy-eight. Vallarsi (t. xi. 8) places his birth still later, in the year 346. His death is placed in the year 419 or 420.

(96; original 354): "Mentiris," said the Lord to him, when Jerome called himself a Christian, Ciceronianus es, non Christianus, ubi enim thesaurus tuus ibi et cor tuum." Ep. xxii. ad Eustochium, "De custodia virginitatis "(tom. i. p. 113). C. A. Heumann has written a special treatise, De ecstasi Hieronymi anti-Ciceroniana. Comp. also Schröckh, vol. vii. p. 35 sqq., and Ozanam: " Civilisation au 5e Siècle," i. 301.

(97; original 355): Ep. xxii. ed. Vall. i. 112).

(98; original 356): Ep. xxii. (i. p. 91, ed. Vallars.)

(99; original 357): Ep. cxxv., ed. Vallars. (al. 95 or 4.)

(100; original 358): De laude vitae solitariae, Ep. xiv. (tom. i. 28-36) ad Heliodorum. The Roman lady Fabiola learned this letter by heart, and Du Pin calls it a masterpiece of eloquence (Nouv. Bibl. des auteurs eccl. iii. 102), but it is almost too declamatory and turgid. He himself afterward acknowledged it overdrawn.

(101; original 359): Gibbon says of them: "The stories of Paul, Hilarion, and Malchus are admirably told; and the only defect of these pleasing compositions is the want of truth and common sense."

(102; original 360): Ep. xiv. (t. i. 29 sq.) Similar descriptions of the attractions of monastic life we meet with in the ascetic writings of Gregory, Basil, Ambrose, Chrysostom, Cassian, Nilus, and Isidor. "So great grace," says the venerable monk Nilus of Mount Sinai, in the beginning of the fifth century (Ep. lib. i Ep. 1, as quoted by Neander, Am. ed. ii. 250), "so great grace his God bestowed on the monks, even in anticipation of the future world, that they wish for no honors from men, and feel no longing after the greatness of this world; but, on the contrary, often seek rather to remain concealed from men: while, on the other hand, many of the great, who possess all the glory of the world, either of their own accord, or compelled by misfortune, take refuge with the lowly monks, and, delivered from fatal dangers, obtain at once a temporal and an eternal salvation."

(103; original 361): As we infer from a remark of Jerome in Ep. cxxiii. c. 10, written a. 409 (ed. Vallars. i. p. 901): "Ante annos plurimos, quum in chartis ecclesiasticis" (i.e. probably in ecclesiastical documents; though Schröckh, viii. p. 122, refers it to the Holy Scriptures, appealing to a work of Bonamici unknown to me), "juvarem Damasum, Romanae urbis episcopum, et orientis atque occidentis synodicis consultationibus responderem," etc. The latter words, which Schröckh does not quote, favor the common interpretation.

(104; original 362): Montalembert, himself the scion of an old noble family in France, l.c. i. p. 388 sq. Comp. Hieron., Epist. lxvi. ad Pammachium, de obit. Paulinae (ed. Vallars. i. 391 sqq.).

(105; original 363): In one of his Epist. ad Pammach.: "Primus inter monachos in prima urbe ... archistrategos monachorum."

(106; original 364): Ep. xxii. ad Eustochium, "de custodia virginitatis." Even Rufinus was shocked at the profane, nay, almost blasphemous expression, socrus Dei, and asked him from what heathen poet he had stolen it.

(107; original 365): His controversy with Augustine on the interpretation of Gal. ii. 14 is not unimportant as an index of the moral character of the two most illustrous Latin fathers of the church. Jerome saw in the account of the collision between Paul and Peter, in Antioch, an artifice of pastoral prudence, and supposed that Paul did not there reprove the senior apostle in earnest, but only for effect, to reclaim the Jews from their wrong notions respecting the validity of the ceremonial law. Augustine’s delicate sense of truth was justly offended by this exegesis, which, to save the dignity of Peter, ascribed falsehood to Paul, and he expressed his opinion to Jerome, who, however, very loftily made him feel his smaller grammatical knowledge. But they afterward became reconciled. Comp. on this dispute the letters on both sides, in Hieron. Opera, ed. Vall. tom. i. 632 sqq., and the treatise of Möhler, in his "Vermischte Schriften," vol. i. p. 1-18.

(108; original 366): "Tantis de toto orbe confluentibus obruimur turbis monachorum."

(109; original 367): The Jesuit Stilting, the author of the Vita Hieron. in the Acta Sanctorum, devotes nearly thirty folio pages to accounts of the veneration paid to him and his relics after his death.

(110; original 368): Most Roman Catholic biographers, as Martianay, Vallarsi, Stilting, Dolci, and even the Anglican Cave, are unqualified eulogists of Jerome. See also the "Selecta Veterum testimonia de Hieronymo ejusque scriptis," in Vallarsi’s edition, tom. xi. pp. 282-300. Tillemont, however, who on account of his Jansenist proclivity sympathizes more with Augustine, makes a move toward a more enlightened judgment, for which Stilting sharply reproves him. Montalembert (l.c. i. 402) praises him as a man of genius, inspired by zeal and subdued by penitence, of ardent faith and immense resources of knowledge; yet he incidentally speaks also of his "almost savage impetuosity of temper," and "that inexhaustible vehemence which sometimes degenerated into emphasis and affectation." Dr. John H. Newman, in his opinion before his transition from Puseyism to Romanism, exhibits the conflict in which the moral feeling is here involved with the authority of the Roman Church: "I do not scruple to say, that, were he not a saint, there are things in his writings and views from which I should shrink; but as the case stands, I shrink rather from putting myself in opposition to something like a judgment of the catholic(?) world in favor of his saintly perfection." (Church of the Fathers, 263, cited by Robertson.) Luther also here boldly broke through tradition, but, forgetful of the great value of the Vulgate even to his German version of the Bible, went to the opposite extreme of unjust derogation, expressing several times a distinct antipathy to this church father, and charging him with knowing not how to write at all of Christ, but only of fasts, virginity, and useless monkish exercises. Le Clerc exposed his defects with thorough ability, but unfairly, in his "Quaestiones Hieronymianae" (Amstel. 1700, over 500 pages). Mosheim and Schröckh are more mild, but the latter considers it doubtful whether Jerome did Christianity more good than harm. Among later Protestant historians opinion has become somewhat more favorable, though rather to his learning than to his moral character, which betrays in his letters and controversial writings too many unquestionable weaknesses.

(111; original 369): Her father professed to trace his genealogy to Agamemnon, and her husband to Aeneas.

(112; original 370): This want of cleanliness, the inseparable companion of ancient ascetic holiness, is bad enough in monks, but still more intolerable and revolting in nuns.

(113; original 371): "Nesciebat se matrem," says Jerome, "ut Christi probaret ancillam." Revealing the conflict of monastic sanctity with the natural virtues which God has enjoined. Montalembert, also, quotes this objectionable passage with apparent approbation.

(114; original 372): Jerome says, Eustochium hoped to pay the debts of her mother—probably by the help of others. Fuller justly remarks: "Liberality should have banks, as well as a stream."

(115; original 373): In Latin Sublaqueum, or Sublacum, in the States of the Church, over thirty English miles (Butler says "near forty," Montalembert, ii. 7, "fifty miles") east of Rome, on the Teverone. Butler describes the place as "a barren, hideous chain of rocks, with a river and lake in the valley."

(116; original 374): Monasterium Cassinense. It was destroyed, indeed, by the Lombards, as early as 583, as Benedict is said to have predicted it would be, but was rebuilt in 731, consecrated in 748, again destroyed by the Saracens in 857, rebuilt about 950, and more completely, after many other calamities, in 1649, consecrated for the third time by Benedict XIII. in 1727, enriched and increased under the patronage of the emperors and popes, but in modern times despoiled of its enormous income (which at the end of the sixteenth century was reckoned at 500,000 ducats), and has stood through all vicissitudes to this day. In the days of its splendor, when the abbot was first baron of the kingdom of Naples, and commanded over four hundred towns and villages, it numbered several hundred monks, but in 1843 only twenty. It has a considerable library. Montalembert (l.c. ii. 19) calls Monte Cassino "the most powerful and celebrated monastery in the Catholic universe; celebrated especially because there Benedict wrote his rule and formed the type which was to serve as a model to innumerable communities submitted to that sovereign code." He also quotes the poetic description from Dante’s Paradiso. Dom Luigi Tosti published at Naples, in 1842, a full history of this convent, in three volumes.

(117; original 375): Gregor. Dial. ii. 37.

(118; original 376): "Scienter nesciens, et sapienter indoctus."

(119; original 377): Butler, l.c., compares him even with Moses and Elijah. "Being chosen by God, like another Moses, to conduct faithful souls into the true promised land, the kingdom of heaven, he was enriched with eminent supernatural gifts, even those of miracles and prophecy. He seemed, like another Eliseus, endued by God with an extraordinary power, commanding all nature, and, like the ancient prophets, foreseeing future events. He often raised the sinking courage of his monks, and baffled the various artifices of the devil with the sign of the cross, rendered the heaviest stone light, in building his monastery, by a short prayer, and, in presence of a multitude of people, raised to life a novice who had been crushed by the fall of a wall at Monte Cassino." Montalembert omits the more extraordinary miracles, except the deliverance of Placidus from the whirlpool, which he relates in the language of Bossuet, ii. 15.

(120; original 378): The Catholic church has recognized three other rules besides that of St. Benedict, viz.: 1. That of St. Basil, which is still retained by the Oriental monks; 2. That of St. Augustine, which is adopted by the regular canons, the order of the preaching brothers or Dominicans, and several military orders; 3. The rule of St. Francis of Assisi, and his mendicant order, in the thirteenth century.

(121; original 379): Pope Gregory believed the rule of St. Benedict even to be directly inspired, and Bossuet (Panégyric de Saint Benoit), in evident exaggeration, calls it "an epitome of Christianity, a learned and mysterious abridgment of all doctrines of the gospel, all the institutions of the holy fathers, and all the counsels of perfection." Montalembert speaks in a similar strain of French declamatory eloquence. Monasticism knows very little of the gospel of freedom, and resolves Christianity into a new law of obedience.

(122; original 380): We have availed ourselves, in this extract from the preface, of the translation of Montalembert, ii. 44 sq.

(123; original 381): Cap. 5: "Primus humilitatis gradus est obedientia sine mora. Haec convenit iis, qui nihil sibi Christo carius aliquid existimant; propter servitium sanctum, quod professi sunt, seu propter metum gehennae, vel gloriam vitae aeternae, mox ut aliquid imperatum a majore fuerit, ac si divinitus imperetur, moram pati nesciunt in faciendo."

(124; original 382): Cap. 48: "Otiositas inimica est animae; et ideo certis temporibus occupari debent fratres in labore manuum, certis iterum horis in lectione divina."

(125; original 383): The horaecanonicae are the Nocturnae vigiliae, Matutinae, Prima, Tertia, Sexta, Nona, Vespera, and Completorium, and are taken (c. 16) from a literal interpretation of Ps. cxix. 164: "Seven times a day do I praise thee," and v. 62: "At midnight I will rise to give thanks unto thee." The Psalter was the liturgy and hymn book of the convent. It was so divided among the seven services of the day, that the whole psalter should be chanted once a week.

(126; original 384): Cap. 59: "Si quis forte de nobilibus offert filium suum Deo in monasterio, si ipse puer minori aetate est, parentes ejus faciant petitionem," etc.

(127; original 385): Cap. 40: "Carnium quadrupedum ab omnibus abstinetur comestio, praeter omnino debiles et aegrotos." Even birds are excluded, which were at that time only delicacies for princes and nobles, as Mabillon shows from the contemporary testimony of Gregory of Tours.

(128; original 386): Cap. 66: "Monasterium, si possit fieri, ita debet construi, ut omnia necessaria, id est, aqua, molendinum, hortus, pistrinum, vel artes diversae intra monasterium exerceantur, ut non sit necessitas monachis vagandi foras, quia omnino non expedit animabus eorum."

(129; original 387): This Maurus, the founder of the abbacy of Glanfeuil (St. Maur sur Loire), is the patron saint of a branch of the Benedictines, the celebrated Maurians in France (dating from 1618), who so highly distinguished themselves in the seventeenth and early part of the eighteenth centuries, by their thorough archaeological and historical researches, and their superior editions of the Fathers. The most eminent of the Maurians are D. (Dom, equivalent to Domnus, Sir) Menard, d’Achery, Godin, Mabillon, le Nourry, Martianay, Ruinart, Martene, Montfaucon, Massuet, Garnier, and de la Rue, and in our time Dom Pitra, editor of a valuable collection of patristic fragments, at the cloister of Solesme.

(130; original 388): He was the last of the Roman consuls—an office which Justinian abolished—and was successively the minister of Odoacer, Theodoric, and Athalaric, who made him prefect of the praetorium

(131; original 389): Or Vivaria, so called from the numerous vivaria or fish ponds in that region.

(132; original 390): Comp. Mabillon, Ann. Bened. l. v. c. 24, 27; F. de Ste. Marthe, Vie de Cassiodore, 1684.

(133; original 391): 1 Cor. vii. 26.

(134; original 392): Adv. Jovin. lib. i. c. 40 (Opera, ii. 304): "Et tamen iste formosus monachus, crassus, nitidus, dealbatus, et quasi sponsus semper incedens, aut uxorem ducat ut aequalem virginitatem nuptiis probet; aut, si non duxerit, frustra contra nos verbis agit, cum opere nobiscum sit."

(135; original 393): Augustine says, De haer. c. 82: "Cito ista haeresis oppressa et extincta est;" and Jerome writes of Jovinian, in 406, Adv. Vigilant. c. 1, that, after having been condemned by the authority of the Roman church, he dissipated his mind in the enjoyment of his lusts.

(136; original 394): 1 Cor. vii. 36, 39.

(137; original 395): 1 Tim. iii. 2, 12.

(138; original 396): 1 Tim. v. 14; comp. 1 Tim. ii. 15; Heb. xiii. 4.

(139; original 397): From 1 Cor. vii. 1, for example ("It is good for a man not to touch a woman"), he argues, without qualification, l. i. c. 7 (Opera, ii. 246): "Si bonum est mulierem non tangere, malum est ergo tangere, nihil enim bono contrarium est, nisi malum; si autem malum est, et ignoscitur, ideo conceditur, ne malo quid deterius fiat .... Tolle fornicationem, et non dicet [apostolus], unusquisque uxorem suam habeat."Immediately after this (ii. 247) he argues, from the exhortation of Paul to pray without ceasing, 1 Thess. v. 17: "Si semper orandum est, nunquam ergo conjugio serviendum, quoniam quotiescunque uxori debitum reddo, orare non possum." Such sophistries and misinterpretations evidently proceed upon the lowest sensual idea of marriage, and called forth some opposition even at that age. He himself afterward felt that he had gone too far, and in his Ep. 48 (ed. Vallars. or Ep. 30, ed. Bened.) ad Pammachium, endeavored to save himself by distinguishing between the gymnastic (polemically rhetorical) and the dogmatic mode of writing.

(140; original 398): De bono conj. c. 8: "Duo bona sunt connubium et continentia, quorum alterum est melius."

(141; original 399): 2 Cor. ix. 6.

(142; original 400): At the very beginning of his work against him, he styles him "hominem rusticum et vix primis quoque imbutum literis."

(143; original 401): Comp. Matt. xxviii. 20.

(144; original 402): Luther, for instance (who even calls Helvidius a "gross fool"), and Zuingle, among the Reformers; Olshausen and J. P. Lange, among the later theologians.

(145; original 403): Respecting his descent, compare the diffuse treatise of the tedious but thorough Walch, l.c. p. 675-677.

(146; original 404): This cheap pun he repeats, Epist. 109, ad Ripar. (Opera, i. p. 719), where he says that Vigilantius (Wakeful) was so called kat j ajntivfrasin, and should rather be called Dormitantius (Sleepy). The fact is, that Vigilantius was wide-awake to a sense of certain superstitions of the age

(147; original 405): In South Gaul; now Casères in Gascogne. As the business of innkeeper is incompatible with the spiritual office, it has been supposed that the father of Vigilantius was a caupo Calagurritanus. Comp. Rössler’s Bibliothek der Kirchenväter, part ix. p. 880 sq., note 100; and Walch, l.c

(148; original 406): Adv. Vigil.c. 1 and 2 (Opera, tom. ii. p. 387 sqq.).

(149; original 407): "Cinerarios et idolatras, qui mortuorum ossa venerantur." Hieron. Ep. 109, ad Riparium (tom. i. p. 719).

(150; original 408): Epiph. Haer. 75. Comp. also Walch, l.c. iii. 321-338. Bellarmine, on account of this external resemblance, styles Protestantism the Aerian heresy.

  1. 参见以下著作中偶尔提到印度裸体哲人的记述：斯特拉波（Strabo），第XV卷第1章（依据亚历山大大帝时代以来的记载）；阿里安（Arrian），《亚历山大远征记》（Exped. Alex.），第VII卷第1—3章，以及《印度史》（Hist. Ind.），第11章；普林尼（Plinius），《自然史》（Hist. Nat.），VII. 2；西西里的狄奥多罗斯（Diodorus Siculus），第II卷；普鲁塔克（Plutarch），《亚历山大传》（Alex.），64；波菲利（Porphyry），《论戒食肉类》（De abstinent.），第IV卷；琉善（Lucian），《逃亡者》（Fugit.），7；亚历山大的革利免（Clemens Alex.），《杂学》（Strom.），第I、III卷；奥古斯丁，《上帝之城》（De Civit. Dei），第XIV卷第17章：“Per opacas Indiae solitudines, quum quidam nudi philosophentur, unde gymnosophistae nominantur; adhibent tamen genitalibus tegmina, quibus per caetera membrorum carent;”（在印度幽暗的荒野中，有些人裸体从事哲学，因而被称为裸体哲人；然而他们仍以遮盖物遮住生殖器，身体其余部分则无遮蔽）；另见第XV卷第20章，他在那里否认他们的独身有任何功德，因为这并非“secundum fidem summi boni, qui est Deus”（按照对至善者、即上帝的信仰）。与这些古代描述相符的，还有《佛国记》（Fon Koueki，约400年，M. A. 雷慕沙〔M. A. Rémusat〕译，巴黎，1836年）、马可·波罗（Marco Polo，1280年）、贝尔尼埃（Bernier，1670年）、汉密尔顿（Hamilton，1700年）、帕皮（Papi）、尼布尔（Niebuhr）、奥尔利希（Orlich）、松纳拉（Sonnerat）等人的记述。↩︎

  2. 印度语称之为tapas，即烧尽，或个体存在的消灭，并融入梵（Brahma）的本体。↩︎

  3. Ad. 武特克（Ad. Wuttke），见其精辟而富于教益的著作《中国人、日本人与印度人的精神生活》（Das Geistesleben der Chinesen, Japaner, und Indier，其《异教史》的第二部），1853年，第593页。↩︎

  4. 参见平克顿（Pinkerton）《航海与旅行集》（Collection of Voyages and Travels）第VII卷所收天主教赴西藏传教士的早期记述，以及法国遣使会传教神父古伯察（Huc）的近著《1844—1846年鞑靼、西藏及中国旅行回忆录》（Souvenirs d’un Voyage dans la Tartarie, le Thibet, et la Chine, pendant les années 1844-1846）。关于整个题目，亦参见R. S. 哈代（Hardy）的两部著作：《东方修道主义》（Eastern Monachism）和《佛教现代发展手册，译自僧伽罗文手稿》（A Manual of Buddhism in its modern development, translated from Singalese MSS.），伦敦，1850年。顺带一提，佛教与罗马天主教之间惊人的相似，不只限于修士制度和修院生活，还延伸至以大喇嘛为教皇的教阶组织，以及礼拜中的仪式、节期、游行、朝圣、告解、一种弥撒、为死者祷告、临终傅油等等。伟大的地理学家卡尔·里特尔（Carl Ritter）曾以自己的声望支持一种看法（《地理学》〔Erdkunde〕，第II卷，第283—299页，第2版），认为西藏喇嘛教徒的宗教形式与仪式，有一部分借自景教传教士；这种看法至少确实颇为有理。但这不过是一种假说，而且有一事实使它显得不大可能：在从未有景教传教士到过的交趾支那（Cochin China）、东京（Tonquin）和日本，佛教也显出与罗马天主教同样惊人的相似，正如西藏、鞑靼和中国北部的喇嘛教一样。关于约在十一世纪兴起的东方亚洲祭司王约翰（Prester John），即基督教祭司君王的奇异传说，以及景教的传教事业，参见里特尔，前引书。↩︎

  5. 参见《民数记》六章1—21节。↩︎

  6. 参见老普林尼（Pliny）对这些犹太修士的非凡描述，《自然史》（Hist. Natur.），V. 15：“Gens sola, et in toto orbe praeter caeteros mira, sine ulla femina, omni venere abdicata, sine pecunia, socia palmarum. Ita per seculorum millia (incredibile dictu) gens aeterna est in qua nemo nascitur. Tam foecunda illis aliorum vitae penitentia est.”（这是一个独特的群体，在全世界诸族中尤为奇异：没有一个女子，弃绝一切情欲，没有钱财，与棕榈树为伴。如此历经数千年〔说来难以置信〕，一个没有人生育的群体竟长存不息。别人对自己生活的悔恨，为他们带来了如此丰盛的生息。）↩︎

  7. 优西比乌斯（Eusebius），《教会史》（H. E.），II. 17，误将他们当作基督徒。↩︎

  8. H. 鲁夫纳（H. Ruffner），前引书，第I卷第II—IX章，详细描述了这些基督教以外的修道形式，并认为基督教的修道主义源出于它们，尤其是佛教的修道主义。↩︎

  9. 连加尔文（Calvin）也是如此；他在《提摩太前书》四章3节的注释中，将保罗关于禁欲式背道的预言主要指向禁欲派（Encratites）、诺斯替派（Gnostics）、孟他努派（Montanists）和摩尼教徒（Manichaeans），但也将它应用于教皇派，“quando coelibatum et ciborum abstinentiam severius urgent quam ullum Dei praeceptum”（当他们强求独身与禁戒食物，比强求上帝的任何诫命还严厉时）。近来的鲁夫纳也是如此，尤其是Is. 泰勒（Is. Taylor），他在《古代基督教》（Ancient Christianity），第I卷第299页及后，专设一章讨论“所预言的禁欲式背道”。然而，最优秀的现代释经家一致认为，使徒所针对的是异端诺斯替派二元论的禁欲主义，它禁止婚姻及某些食物，认为它们本质上不洁；而罗马与希腊教会却以婚姻为圣礼，只是将它置于独身之下，并把禁婚的范围限于教士和修士。因此，将《提摩太前书》四章1—3节应用于天主教会，至多只能在部分而间接的意义上成立。↩︎

  10. 参见第I卷，§ 94—97。↩︎

  11. 拉克坦提乌斯（Lactantius）说，连呼吸的自由也得花钱购买；据佐西穆斯（Zosimus，《历史》〔Hist.〕，II. 38）记述，父亲们让自己的女儿卖淫，以筹得纳税的钱。↩︎

  12. 源自ἀναχωρέω*，即退隐（离开人类社会），ἀναχωρητής*、ἐρημίτης*（源自ἐρημία*，意为荒野）。μοναχός*一词（源自μόνος*，独自，以及μονάζειν*，独居），即monachus（monk〔修士〕由此而来），原来也指孤独的隐士生活，但通常与共住修士或托钵修士同义。↩︎

  13. Κοινόβιον*，coenobium；源自κοινός βίος*，vita communis，即共同生活；后来指修士的团体，有时也指建筑物。μάνδρα*（畜栏、羊圈）及μοναστήριον*、claustrum（cloister〔修院〕由此而来）也有同样的意思。另有λαύραι*，laurae（字面意为街道），即小室；通常在相距不远之处建起多间，形成一个小村落。因此，这个词常与monasterium（修道院）同义。不过，单数形式λαῦρα*则对应于隐修生活。关于修道主义的这些名称，参见杜康日（Du Cange）《中古及晚期拉丁语词典》（Glossarium mediae et infimae Latinitatis）各相应词条。↩︎

  14. Ἡγούμενος*、ἀρχεμανδρίτης*、ἀββᾶς*，即父亲，abbot（修道院院长）由此而来。女主管在叙利亚语中称为ἀμμᾶς*，即母亲、女修道院院长。↩︎

  15. 源自nonna，即casta，贞洁、圣洁。这个词可能源于科普特语，早在哲罗姆的著作中便已出现。阳性形式nonnus，即修士，在中世纪屡见不鲜。参见杜康日相应词条中的例证。↩︎

  16. 因此，米德尔顿（Middleton）不无道理地说：“根据我读到的有关古代修士的一切，以及我亲眼所见的现代修士，我认为后者显然更值得推重，因为他们比前者有更整饬的纪律、更多良好的学识和更少的迷信。”↩︎

  17. 按拿先斯的贵格利（Gregory Nazianzen）等人的说法，是Βίος θεωρητικός*与βίος πρακτικός*。整个中世纪都以拉撒路的两个姊妹来说明vita contemplativa（默观生活）与vita activa（行动生活）的区别，见《路加福音》十章38—42节。↩︎

  18. Ἡ κατὰ θεὸν*或Χριστὸν φιλοσοφία*，ἡ ὑψηλὴ φιλος.*，即按古人的意义，与其说是一种思辨体系，不如说是一种受特定准则约束的生活方式。毕达哥拉斯派（Pythagoreans）、斯多亚派（Stoics）、犬儒派（Cynics）及新柏拉图派（Neo-Platonists）都是如此。禁欲者与哲学家是同一回事。↩︎

  19. Ἀποστολικὸς βίος*，ὁ τῶν ἀγγέλων βίος*，vita angelica（天使般的生活）；这是对基督论天使无性别生活的话所作的不当应用，见《马太福音》二十二章30节。那里并未将这种生活提出作为效法的榜样，只是用作反驳撒都该人的论据。↩︎

  20. 见他著名的战诗《巴拉克拉瓦轻骑兵冲锋》（The Charge of the Light Brigade at Balaclava），1854年初版。↩︎

  21. 参见十二世纪沙特尔的伊夫（Yves de Chartres）切实的评语，《书信》（Ep.）192（蒙塔朗贝尔〔Montalembert〕引）：“Non beatum faciunt hominem secreta sylvarum, cacumina montium, si secum non habet solitudinem mentis, sabbatum cordis, tranquillitatem conscientiae, ascensiones in corde, sine quibus omnem solitudinem comitantur mentis acedia, curiositas, vana gloria, periculosae tentationum procellae.”（幽林深处与山巅不能使人蒙福，除非他自身具有心灵的独处、内心的安息、良心的平静和心中的上升；没有这些，无论在哪里独处，都有精神的懈怠、好奇、虚荣和危险的试探风暴相随。）↩︎

  22. 哲罗姆就是如此，见《致保利努斯书》（ad Paulinum），《书信》（Ep.）49（本笃会版〔ed. Ben.〕）；他在那里除以利亚和约翰之外，也举以赛亚和先知门徒为修道主义之父。又见他的《保罗传》（Vita Pauli）；不过，他在那里较为准确地将底比斯的保罗（Paul of Thebes）和安东尼（Anthony）称为最早的、严格意义上的隐士，以区别于先知。亦参见索佐门（Sozomen），《教会史》（H. E.），第I卷第12章：Ταύτης δὲ τῆς ἀρίστης φιλοσοφίας ἤρξατο, ὥς τινες λέγουσιν, Ἡλίας ὁ προφήτης καὶ Ἰωάννης ὁ βαπτιστής.*。天主教作者论及这一题目时，援引以利亚和施洗约翰的榜样，已成为传统。阿尔班·巴特勒（Alban Butler）在1月15日的底比斯的保罗传中说：“以利亚和施洗约翰使旷野成圣，耶稣基督自己在旷野禁食四十天期间，也作了隐修生活的榜样；同样无可置疑的是，圣灵引导本日纪念的圣徒（底比斯的保罗）进入旷野，并在那里作他的导师。”↩︎

  23. 因此称为consilia evangelica（福音劝谕），以区别于mandata divina（神圣命令）；依据是《哥林多前书》七章25节，保罗在那里确实作了类似的区分。consilium与votum paupertatis（贫穷的劝谕与誓愿）以《马太福音》十九章21节为根据；votum castitatis（贞洁誓愿）以《哥林多前书》七章8、25、38—40节为根据。至于votum obedientiae（顺服誓愿），则未引任何特定经文。这一理论的实质早在俄利根（Origen）那里便已出现；在他那里，这不只是个人意见，也是极为普遍的实践的反映。参见第I卷，§ 94、95。↩︎

  24. 参见《歌罗西书》二章16—23节。↩︎

  25. 参见《哥林多前书》十三章1—3节。参见第168页及下页。↩︎

  26. Ἄπλαστος*，字面意为未经塑造的；因而意为自然的、真诚的。↩︎

  27. Μὰ τὸν Ἰησοῦν*（萨尔维安〔Salvian〕作 per Christum，即“指着基督”），如今取代了异教的誓语：μὰ τὸν Δία*，即“指着朱庇特起誓”。↩︎

  28. 见狄奥多勒（Theodoret）：《修士史》(Hist. relig.)，第13章（传）。↩︎

  31. 《教会史》(Hist. Eccles.)，第vi卷，第34章。↩︎

  32. 后者本身也不是完全赤裸的，而是在腰间有所遮蔽；奥古斯丁（Augustine）在上引《上帝之城》(De Civit. Dei)第xiv卷第17章中，以及后来的旅行者，都如此告诉我们。另一方面，有些修士在这一点上却极其谨慎。据说亚扪（Ammon）从未看见过自己赤裸的身体。按帕科米乌斯（Pachomius）的规条，塔本奈（Tabennae）的修士必须始终穿着衣服睡觉。↩︎

  34. 即βοσκοί*，或 pabulatores（食草者）。参见索佐门（Sozom.），《教会史》(H. E.)，第vi卷，33章。叙利亚的以法莲（Ephraim Syrus）曾专门为他们作了一篇颂辞，见蒂耶蒙（Tillemont），《回忆录》(Mem.)，第viii卷，第292页及下页所引。↩︎

  35. 《修士史》(Hist. Rel.)，第xv章（传）（《全集》〔Opera omnia〕，巴黎版〔ed Par.〕，第iii卷，第843页及后）。↩︎

  36. 《对话录》(Dial.)，第i卷，第8章。塞韦鲁斯（Severus）在这件事上看见了基督制伏野兽之能力的奇妙实例。↩︎

  38. 见鲁夫纳（Ruffner），前引书，第i卷，第49页及后；以及武特克（Wuttke），前引书，第369页及后。↩︎

  39. 这是按照对《以弗所书》六章12节的一种感官化、空间化的理解：Τὰ πνευματικὰ τῆς πονηρίας ἐν τοῖς ἐπουρανίοις*；路德（Luther）译为“die bösen Geister unter dem Himmel”（天底下的邪灵）；《武加大译本》则译得直白，却略显晦涩：“Spiritualia nequitiae in coelestibus”（天上邪恶的属灵势力）；英文圣经译作“Spiritual wickedness in high places”（高处属灵的邪恶），又过于自由了。无论如何，πνευματικά*的意义都应比πνεύματα*或δαιμόνια*宽泛得多；而ἐπουράνια*也不完全等同于云天或大气层，并且还容许另一种句法解释，因此许多人在πονηρίας*之后加上逗号。我们可以指出，修道主义的撒但论和鬼魔论，在古代教会及整个中世纪都普遍受到接受。众所周知，路德也从他的修道生活中保留了一种关于魔鬼及其对人之影响的感官化、物质化观念。↩︎

  40. 亚他那修（Athanasius）说，魔鬼有时以女人的形貌向圣安东尼（Anthony）显现；哲罗姆（Jerome）叙述，圣希拉里翁（Hilarion）在床上时，他的想象常受裸体女人的幻象侵扰。哲罗姆自己在致一位童贞女子（！）的信中承认，《书信》(Epist.)第xxii封（瓦拉尔西版〔ed. Vallars.〕，第i卷，第91、92页），致欧斯托基乌姆（Eustochium）的《论守贞》(de Custodia Virginitatis)：“O quoties ego ipse in eremo constitutus et in illa vasta solitudine, quae exusta solis ardoribus horridum monachis praebebat habitaculum, putavi me Romanis interesse deliciis .... Ille igitur ego, qui ob gehennae metum tali me carcere ipse damnaveram, scorpionum tantum socius et ferarum, saepe choris intereram puellarum. Pallebant ora jejuniis, et mens desideriis aestuabat in frigido corpore, et ante hominem suum jam in carne praemortuum, sola libidinum incendia bulliebant. Itaque omni auxilio destitutus, ad Jesu jacebam pedes, rigabam lacrymis, crine tergebam et repugnantem carnem hebdomadarum inedia subjugabam.”（我身在旷野，在那被烈日灼烧、为修士提供可怕居所的广大荒漠中，有多少次竟以为自己正置身罗马的奢乐之中！……我因惧怕地狱而自囚于这样的牢狱，只与蝎子和野兽为伴，却常仿佛置身少女的舞群之间。我的脸因禁食而苍白，冰冷的身体里面，心灵却被欲望烧灼；肉身中的人虽已近乎未死先亡，唯有情欲的烈焰仍在沸腾。因此，我无援无助，伏在耶稣脚前，以泪水浇湿他的脚，用头发擦干，又以数周的绝食制伏反抗的肉体。）圣以法莲（Ephraim）警告人不要听从仇敌；仇敌向修士低声说道：Οὐ δυνατὸν παύσασθει ἀπό σου, ἐὰν μὴ πληροφορήσῃς ἐπιθυμίαν σου*。↩︎

  41. 《帕科米乌斯传》(Vita Pach.)，§ 61。参见尼卢斯（Nilus），《书信》(Epist.)，第ii卷，第140页：Τινὲς ... ἑαυτοὺς ἔσφαξαν μαχαίρᾳ*，等等。甚至在非洲多纳徒派（Donatist）的狂热托钵修士，即巡游者派（Circumcelliones）中，自杀也并不罕见。↩︎

  42. 蒂耶蒙（Tillem.），第vii卷，第430页。据蒂耶蒙叙述，院长随后从启示中得知，“que Muce avait egalé par son obeissance celle d’Abraham”（缪斯〔Muce〕的顺服已可与亚伯拉罕的顺服相比），不久便立他为自己的继任者。↩︎

  43. Omnino monachum fugere debere mulieres et episcopos.（修士务必远避女人和主教。）↩︎

  44. 索佐门（Sozom.），第iv卷，30章。↩︎

  45. 《（古代）基督教史》(Hist. of (ancient) Christianity)，美国版，第432页。↩︎

  46. 夏多布里昂（Chateaubriand）主要从第一种观点赞扬修道制度。“既然有维护身体健康的避难所，啊！也请容许宗教为灵魂的健康设立这样的避难所吧；灵魂更容易患病，它的疾病也更令人悲伤，更加缠绵，更难治愈！”蒙塔朗贝尔（Montalembert，前引书，第i卷，第25页）反对这一观点，认为它虽富有诗意、动人心弦，却并不真实；他把修道生活描述为世间所产生的最健康、最强健的灵魂的竞技场，并引用金口约翰（Chrysostom）的话：“来看看基督精兵的帐幕吧；来看看他们的战阵吧；他们天天争战，天天击败并宰杀那些侵袭我们的情欲。”↩︎

  47. 1 佩卢西乌姆的伊西多尔（Isidore of Pelusium）院长曾写信给一位奴隶主，《书信》(Ep.)，第i卷，142封（尼安德〔Neander〕引）：“我不曾想到，一个爱基督、并且知道那使我们众人得自由之恩典的人，竟还会蓄奴。”↩︎

  48. 耶稣会士罗斯韦德（Rosweyde）的《教父传》(Vitae Patrum)和《圣徒行传》(Acta Sanctorum)中，充斥着修士的神迹，往往违反自然与理性的一切法则；若非出自哲罗姆（Jerome）、鲁菲努斯（Rufinus）、塞韦鲁斯（Severus）、帕拉迪乌斯（Palladius）和狄奥多勒（Theodoret）这样的教父之手，并有助于表现尼西亚时代的特征，它们几乎不值得一提。我们远不愿将这一切一概斥为谎言和欺骗，也不愿接受艾萨克·泰勒（Isaac Taylor）的判断（《古代基督教》〔Ancient Christianity〕，第ii卷，第106页）：“尼西亚时代的神迹，无不冒犯人对庄重、体面和虔敬的一切感情；从其显明的特征来看，它们幼稚、可怖、亵渎而污秽。”罗伯逊（Robertson，《基督教会史》〔Hist. of the Christian Church〕，第i卷，第312页）和其他新教史家的意见要谨慎得多；他们认为，除了过度亢奋的想象所产生的无心幻觉，以及蓄意欺骗所编造的故事之外，也必定有许多真实的事情，尽管按这类事情的性质，不可能作出精确的甄别。然而，其中许多故事甚至连罗马天主教的轻信也难以容纳，因此，批判性的史家或将它们完全略去，或至少大加删削和修改。我们所读到的，不仅是无数异象、预言、医治病人和被鬼附者的事，还有使死人复活（如图尔的马丁〔Martin of Tours〕传中所记）、枯杖长成果树，以及一名修士绝对服从院长的命令，穿过火炉而毫发无伤，如同经过清凉的浴池。（参见苏尔皮基乌斯·塞韦鲁斯〔Sulp. Sever.〕，《对话录》〔Dial.〕，第i卷，第12、13章。）甚至野兽也占了很大篇幅，被变成了埃及沙漠圣徒们有理性的仆役。据哲罗姆说，在底比斯的保罗（Paul of Thebes）的葬礼上，两头狮子自愿担任掘墓人的职务。帕科米乌斯（Pachomius）践踏蛇蝎而不受伤害，又乘着鳄鱼渡过尼罗河；鳄鱼主动将背脊献给他。小马卡里乌斯（Macarius），或据《劳苏斯史》（Historia Lausiaca）的其他记述，是修士马可（Marcus）（参见蒂耶蒙〔Tillemont〕的考证，第viii卷，第811页及后），与野兽相处得如此融洽，以致有一只鬣狗（据鲁菲努斯，V. P.，第ii卷，4章，则是一头母狮）将幼崽带到他的斗室里，请他使幼崽的眼睛睁开；他便祷告，又涂上唾沫，治好了它。第二天，鬣狗为表谢意，送给他一张大羊皮；圣徒起初拒绝了这礼物，并责备这野兽为取得羊皮而犯下杀戮和偷盗的双重罪行；但鬣狗表示悔改，点头应允改过之后，马卡里乌斯便收下羊皮，后来将它遗赠给伟大的主教亚他那修（Athanasius）。塞韦鲁斯（《对话录》，第i卷，第9章）对一位无名隐修士也有极相似的记述，不过他像鲁菲努斯一样，以一头带着五只幼崽的母狮取代帕拉迪乌斯所说的鬣狗，并说圣徒收下所赠的兽皮，既未迟疑，也未责备。可是在稍前之处（第8章），他讲到一只狼；它素来与一位友善的隐修士共进晚餐，有一次偷了他的东西，便深深悔恨，垂头请求圣徒饶恕。或许帕拉迪乌斯或他的拉丁文译者把这两则轶事合在了一起。↩︎

  49. 普林尼（Pliny）列举了三十九种不同的棕榈树，其中最好的生长在埃及，四季常青，枝叶繁茂，所结的果实在有些地方被用来制作饼。↩︎

  50. 哲罗姆（Jerome）在《底比斯的保罗传》(Vita Pauli Theb.)第i章中论安东尼（Anthony）说：“Non tam ipse auto omnes (eremitas) fuit, quam ab eo omnium incitata sunt studia.”（与其说他本人早于所有隐修士，不如说所有人的热忱都是由他激发的。）↩︎

  51. 按通常的看法，也是奥古斯丁（Augustine）的看法，安东尼（Anthony）甚至不识字。但蒂耶蒙（Tillemont，第vii卷，第107、666页）、巴特勒（Butler）等人认为，他所不识的只是希腊字母，而非埃及文字。亚他那修（Athanasius）在第795页说得有些含混：他因为害怕与人交往，不愿μαθεῖν γράμματα*（学习文字？还是学艺？），但后来又说到他对阅读的重视。↩︎

  52. 奥古斯丁（Augustine）在《论基督教教义》(De doctr. Christ.)，§ 4中论他说，他虽不识字，却仅凭聆听圣经便将其熟记于心。亚他那修（Athanasius）所作的传记确实表明，他对许多经文十分熟悉。但无论在他身上，还是在这些隐修士的一般情形中，我们都找不到连贯而深入的圣经知识的痕迹。↩︎

  53. 《马太福音》十九章21节。↩︎

  54. 《马太福音》六章34节。↩︎

  55. 亚他那修（Athanasius）说，Εἰς παρθενῶνα*；这并非蒂耶蒙（Tillemont）所译的“un monastere de verges”（一所童贞女子的修道院），因为当时尚无女修道院；而是指会众内部的女性禁欲者团体。不过，这样的团体当然很容易发展成为正式的修道院。↩︎

  56. 哲罗姆（Jerome）在《希拉里翁传》(Vita Hilarionis)第31章中，顺带描述了安东尼（Anthony）这最后的居所；照他的描述，那里并不像人从亚他那修（Athanasius）的记述所推想的那样荒凉。他甚至说，这园中有棕榈、果树和葡萄树，其果实是任何人都会乐于享用的。↩︎

  57. 亚他那修（Athanas.），第42章：Ἐλθόντες γὰρ (οἱ ἐχθροὶ) ὁποίους ἀν εὕρωσιν ἡμᾶς*，τοιοῦτοι καὶ αὐτοὶ γίνονται*，等等——这是一项重要的心理学观察。↩︎

  63. 鲁菲努斯（Rufinus，《教父传》〔Vitae Patr.〕，第ii卷，第7章）说：“Quanti populi habentur in urbibus, tantae paene habentur in desertis multitudines monachorum.”（城里有多少人口，旷野中几乎就有多少修士。）吉本（Gibbon）加上一句讥讽的话：“后人或许可以重提那句从前用来形容同一国度之神圣动物的话：在埃及，找一个神比找一个人还容易。”蒙塔朗贝尔（Montalembert，《西方修士》〔Monks of the West〕，第i卷，第314页）论修士人数的增长说：“在这些埃及隐修士的奇妙历史中，没有什么比他们的人数更令人难以置信了。然而，最有分量的权威都一致确认了这一事实（圣奥古斯丁，De morib. Eccles.，第i卷，31章）。这是一种从城市向旷野、从文明向简朴、从喧嚣向寂静、从败坏向纯真的迁徙。潮流一经兴起，成群的男人、女人和儿童便投入其中，以不可抗拒之势向那里涌去，历时一个世纪。”↩︎

  64. 有好几位（五位或七位）隐修士都用这个名字，常被混淆。其中最著名的是老马卡里乌斯（Macarius），又称大马卡里乌斯（† 390），那些《讲道》很可能出自他手；以及亚历山大的小马卡里乌斯（† 404），他是帕拉迪乌斯（Palladius）的老师；帕拉迪乌斯与他长期相处，对他的评价与对前者同样高。参见蒂耶蒙（Tillemont）的详述，第viii卷，第574—650页；以及注释，第811页及后。↩︎

  69. 至少在基督教会中是首创。吉泽勒（Gieseler）提到一个异教的先例：卢奇安（Lucian）在《论叙利亚女神》(De Dea Syria)第28、29章中提及的叙利亚Φαλλοβατεῖς*。↩︎

  70. 然而，巴特勒（Butler）在前引书中，也记述了当代一位本笃会修士克洛德·莱昂特修士（Dom Claude Leante）的类似事迹：“1731年，他约五十一岁，已经连续十一年在大斋期禁食，整整四十天除每日弥撒时所领受的以外，不进任何食物；更令人惊奇的是，大斋期中他并不真正睡觉，只是打盹。他受不了户外的空气；到了大斋期将尽时，他面色极其苍白，身体消瘦。这件事有他的弟兄和上级作证，见1731年在桑斯（Sens）刊印的一篇记述。”↩︎

  71. 他亲自竖立、并在上面生活了四年的第一根柱子，高六肘（πήχεων*）；第二根高十二肘，第三根二十二肘，第四根由民众为他竖立，他在上面度过了二十年。据狄奥多雷（Theodoret）说，第四根高三十六肘；另有人说是四十肘。柱顶直径仅三英尺。不过，柱顶大概装有栏杆，使他在睡眠或疲乏时可以倚靠。至少图画中的这些柱子都是这样描绘的。门徒沿梯子把食物送上去，供给柱上圣徒。↩︎

  75. 默勒（Möhler）对此评论说（《杂著》〔Vermischte Schriften〕，ii，第183页）：“古代以此表达其信念：对于与上帝完全和好的人，自然界中没有仇敌。这里不只是诗意；至少也表达了帕科米乌斯（Pachomius）同时代及后世的人对他的崇高评价。”末尾这一限定语表明，即使这位著名的近代罗马天主教捍卫者，心中也对这位修道圣徒的神奇故事是否确属史实存有疑问。↩︎

  76. 纳齐安佐的贵格利（Gregory Nazianzen）在颂扬巴西流（Basil）的演说中（旧编次第xx篇演说，新巴黎版第xliii篇演说），称赞他努力在修道生活中将默观与实践两种生活方式结合起来，ἵνα μήτε τὸ φιλόσοφον ἀκοινώνητον ᾖ, μήτε τὸ πρακτικὸν ἀφιλόσοφον*，意为“使哲思的生活不至于脱离群体，实践的生活也不至于缺少哲思”。↩︎

  80. 苏尔皮基乌斯·塞韦鲁斯（Sulpitius Severus）在其三篇对话的第一篇中，举了几个有趣的例子，说明高卢人与埃及人的胃口有何不同。他所拜访的第一位埃及隐修士，给他和四位同伴摆上半个大麦面包和一把菜蔬作正餐，令他大为惊讶；不过，经过疲惫的旅途之后，这些食物吃起来倒很可口。他说：“Edacitas, in Graecis gula est, in Gallia natura.”（“贪食在希腊人中是口腹之欲，在高卢却是天性。”）（《对话录》〔Dial.〕，i，第8章，载加兰迪〔Gallandi〕，第viii卷，第405页。）↩︎

  81. 蒙塔朗贝尔（Montalembert）在前引书中说：“修道主义这条河流发源于埃及的沙漠，分成两条大支流。一条流向东方，起初淹没一切，后来收缩，最终消失在那里。另一条流入西方，沿着千百条水道遍及整个世界；这个世界需要它来浸润，使之肥沃。”↩︎

  82. 奥古斯丁（Augustine），《忏悔录》(Conf.)，vii. 6：“Erat monasterium Mediolani plenum bonis fratribus extra urbis moenia, sub Ambrosio nutritore.”（“米兰城墙外有一所修道院，满是善良的弟兄，由安布罗斯抚育。”）↩︎

  83. 安布罗斯（Ambr.）：《论童贞女》(De virginibus)，第iii卷，约377年写给其姊妹玛尔切利娜（Marcellina）。参蒂耶蒙（Tillem.），x. 102—105，以及施勒克（Schröckh），viii，第355页及后。↩︎

  84. 安布罗斯（Ambr.）：《创世六日论》(Hexaëmeron)，第iii卷，第5章。哲罗姆（Hieron.）：《致奥凯阿努斯论法比奥拉之死书》(Ep. ad Oceanum de morte Fabiolae)，瓦拉尔西版（ed. Vall.）第77封信（本笃会版〔ed. Ben.〕第84封，其他编次为第30封）。↩︎

  85. 他自己提到，在主教住所中有一所“教士修道院”（monasterium clericorum）；他的传记作者波西狄乌斯（Possidius）在《传记》(Vita) 第5章中说：“Factus ergo presbyter monasterium inter ecclesiam mox instituit, et cum Dei servis vivere coepit secundum modum, et regulam sub sanctis apostlis constitutam, maxime ut nemo quidquam proprium haberet, sed eis essent omnia communia.”（“因此，他成为长老之后，随即在教会中设立了一所修道院，开始与上帝的仆人共同生活，遵照圣使徒们所确立的方式和规条，尤其是无人拥有私产，一切都归众人公用。”）↩︎

  86. 《论修士的劳作》(De opera monach.)，第22章。更晚些时候，萨尔维安（Salvian）仍谈到非洲人对修道主义的憎恨（《论上帝的治理》〔De gubern. Dei〕，viii. 4）。↩︎

  87. 见他的《马丁传》(Vita Martini)，以及关于马丁的三封信和三篇文笔极其雄辩优美的对话；第一篇对话论东方修士，另两篇论马丁的神迹（拉夫纳〔Ruffner〕《沙漠教父》〔Fathers of the Desert〕第ii卷，第68—178页有译文，略有删节）。他告诉我们（《对话录》〔Dial.〕，i，第23章），罗马书商出售他的《马丁传》，销路超过其他任何书籍，并从中获利甚丰。当时，人们把圣徒行传当作传奇故事来读。↩︎

  88. 传记作者在这里所指的，当然是《马太福音》二十五章40节。↩︎

  89. 见其传记接近结尾处，第26、27章（加兰迪〔Gallandi〕，第viii卷，第399页）。↩︎

  90. 参《对话录》(Dial.)，ii. 5（载加兰迪《文库》〔Gallandi, Bibl.〕，第viii卷，第412页）。↩︎

  91. 《马丁传》(Vita M.)，第20章（载加兰迪〔Gallandi〕，viii，第397页）。↩︎

  92. 《对话录》(Dial.)，ii. 7，大概说的是同一次宴会，因为马丁（Martin）谢绝了皇帝其他的宴请。塞韦鲁斯（Severus）的叙述使我们明白，这是马丁唯一一次容许女人如此接近自己，或接受女人的服侍。他曾称赞一位修女，因为她连他以主教身份作的正式探访也拒绝了；塞韦鲁斯对此评论道：“何等荣耀的童贞女，竟连马丁也不让看见自己！何等有福的马丁，不将这种拒绝视为侮辱，反而赞扬其中的美德，并因在那个地区发现如此罕见的榜样而欢喜！”（《对话录》〔Dial.〕，ii，第12章；加兰迪〔Gall.〕，viii，第414页。）↩︎

  93. 正如他自己在驳鲁菲努斯（Rufinus）的第二篇申辩中所夸耀的：“Ego philosophus(?), rhetor, grammaticus, dialecticus, hebraeus, graecus, latinus, trilinguis.”（“我是哲学家〔？〕、修辞学家、文法学家、辩证学家，通希伯来文、希腊文、拉丁文，精于三种语言。”）著名的伊拉斯谟（Erasmus）是其著作的第一位编者，在文学才具与成就方面也极有鉴赏力。他将哲罗姆置于所有教父之上，甚至高于圣奥古斯丁（他不能赞同奥古斯丁关于白白恩典和预定的教义），并屡次以雄辩的言辞表达对哲罗姆的钦佩。在致教皇利奥十世（Leo X.）的一封信中（《书信》〔Ep.〕，ii. 1，引自瓦拉尔西〔Vallarsi〕版《哲罗姆著作集》，第xi卷，第290页），他说：“Divus Hieronymus sic apud Latinos est theologorum princeps, ut hunc prope solum habeamus theologi dignum nomine. Non quod caeteros damnem, sed quod illustres alioqui, si cum hoc conferantur, ob huius eminentiam velut obscurentur. Denique tot egregiis est cumulatus dotibus, ut vix ullum habeat et ipsa docta Graecia, quem cum hoc viro quest componere. Quantum in illo Romanae facundiae! quanta linguarum peritia! quanta omnis antiquitatis omnium historiarum notitia! quam fida memoria! quam felix rerum omnium mixtum! quam absoluta mysticarum literarum cognitio! super omnia, quis ardor ille, quam admirabilis divini pectoris afflatus? ut una et plurimum delectet eloquentia, et doceat eruditione, et rapiat sanctimonia.”（“圣哲罗姆在拉丁人中实为神学家之首，以致我们几乎认为，只有他才配称为神学家。这并非贬斥其他人，而是他们虽自有光彩，一旦与他相比，却因他的卓越而显得黯淡。他集如此众多的卓越禀赋于一身，以致即使博学的希腊，也难找出一人可与之匹敌。他有何等罗马式的雄辩！何等精湛的语言造诣！何等广博的古代知识和历史知识！何等可靠的记忆！何等融会万事的才华！何等完备的圣经奥义知识！更重要的是，他有何等的热忱，那神圣心灵的感发又何等奇妙！他的雄辩使人极其愉悦，他的博学使人受教，他的圣洁使人心驰神往，而这一切同时具备。”）↩︎

  94. 马尔蒂亚奈（Martianay）、斯蒂尔廷（Stilting）、凯夫（Cave）、施勒克（Schröckh）、哈根巴赫（Hagenbach）等人，根据普罗斯珀（Prosper）《编年史》(Chron.) 331年条，将他的出生年份定为331年；巴罗尼乌斯（Baronius）、杜潘（Du Pin）和蒂耶蒙（Tillemont）则定为342年，后者更为可信。蒂耶蒙根据种种情形推断，哲罗姆并非如普罗斯珀所说活了九十一岁，而仅活了七十八岁。瓦拉尔西（Vallarsi，xi. 8）将其出生年代定得更晚，认为是346年。他的卒年则定为419或420年。↩︎

A. 霍伊曼（C. A. Heumann）曾撰专论《论哲罗姆反西塞罗的神游异象》(De ecstasi Hieronymi anti-Ciceroniana)。又参施勒克（Schröckh），第vii卷，第35页及后；以及奥扎南（Ozanam）：《五世纪的文明》(Civilisation au 5e Siècle)，i. 301。↩︎

  99. 《书信》(Ep.)，第cxxv封，瓦拉尔西版（ed. Vallars.）（其他编次为第95或第4封）。↩︎

  100. 《赞独居生活》(De laude vitae solitariae)，第xiv封信（第i卷，第28—36页），致赫利奥多鲁斯（Heliodorus）。罗马贵妇法比奥拉（Fabiola）将此信熟记于心，杜潘（Du Pin）称之为雄辩的杰作（《教会作家新文库》〔Nouv. Bibl. des auteurs eccl.〕，iii. 102），但它几乎显得过于铺张而浮夸。他自己后来也承认写得过火了。↩︎

  101. 吉本（Gibbon）论到这些作品时说：“保罗、希拉里昂（Hilarion）和马尔库斯（Malchus）的故事讲述得极其精彩；这些悦人的作品唯一的缺点，就是缺少真实性与常识。”↩︎

  102. 《书信》(Ep.)，第xiv封（第i卷，第29页及下页）。贵格利（Gregory）、巴西流（Basil）、安布罗斯（Ambrose）、金口约翰（Chrysostom）、卡西安（Cassian）、尼鲁斯（Nilus）和伊西多尔（Isidor）的禁欲著作中，也有类似描绘修道生活魅力的文字。西奈山德高望重的修士尼鲁斯在五世纪初说（《书信》〔Ep.〕，第i卷，第1封，转引自尼安德〔Neander〕，美国版，ii. 250）：“上帝赐给修士如此丰盛的恩典，甚至使他们预先领略来世，以致他们不求人的尊荣，也不渴慕这世界的显贵；相反，他们往往宁愿避开人的耳目。另一方面，许多享有世间一切荣耀的显贵，或出于自愿，或为患难所迫，投奔卑微的修士，得以脱离致命的危险，同时获得今世与永世的拯救。”↩︎

  103. 这是我们从哲罗姆写于409年的第cxxiii封信第10章中的一句话推断出来的（瓦拉尔西版〔ed. Vallars.〕，i，第901页）：“Ante annos plurimos, quum in chartis ecclesiasticis”（“许多年前，当我在教会文书方面”——这里大概指教会文件；不过施勒克〔Schröckh〕，viii，第122页，援引一本我未见过的博纳米奇〔Bonamici〕的著作，认为是指圣经），“juvarem Damasum, Romanae urbis episcopum, et orientis atque occidentis synodicis consultationibus responderem”（“协助罗马城主教达马苏，并答复东方与西方主教会议的咨询时”），等等。施勒克没有引用的后面这些话，支持通常的解释。↩︎

  104. 蒙塔朗贝尔（Montalembert）本人出身于法国一个古老的贵族家庭，见前引书，i，第388页及下页。参哲罗姆（Hieron.），《致帕马基乌斯论保利娜之死书》(Epist. lxvi. ad Pammachium, de obit. Paulinae)，第lxvi封信（瓦拉尔西版〔ed. Vallars.〕，i，第391页及后）。↩︎

  105. 见他《致帕马基乌斯的书信》(Epist. ad Pammach.) 中的一封：“Primus inter monachos in prima urbe ... archistrategos monachorum.”（“首城中修士之首……修士的统帅。”）↩︎

  106. 第xxii封信，致尤斯托基乌姆（Eustochium），《论守贞》(de custodia virginitatis)。就连鲁菲努斯（Rufinus）也对socrus Dei（“上帝的岳母”）这一亵渎、甚至几近谤渎上帝的说法感到震惊，问他是从哪一位异教诗人那里偷来的。↩︎

  107. 他与奥古斯丁就《加拉太书》二章14节的解释所发生的争论，对于认识这两位最杰出的拉丁教父的道德品格，并非无关紧要。哲罗姆认为，保罗与彼得在安提阿冲突的记述，体现了一种牧养上的权宜之计；他以为保罗当时并非当真责备那位年长的使徒，而只是为了产生效果，使犹太人改正关于礼仪律法效力的错误观念。这种解释为保全彼得的尊严，竟将虚假归给保罗，理所当然地触犯了奥古斯丁敏锐的真理意识。他向哲罗姆表达了自己的看法，哲罗姆却以极其傲慢的态度，使他感受到自己在文法知识上的不足。不过，两人后来和好了。关于这场争论，参双方书信，载《哲罗姆著作集》(Hieron. Opera)，瓦拉尔西版（ed. Vall.），第i卷，第632页及后；以及默勒（Möhler）《杂著》(Vermischte Schriften) 第i卷，第1—18页所收的论文。↩︎

  108. “Tantis de toto orbe confluentibus obruimur turbis monachorum.”（“如此众多的修士从全世界涌来，使我们应接不暇。”）↩︎

  109. 耶稣会士斯蒂尔廷（Stilting）是《圣徒行传》(Acta Sanctorum) 中《哲罗姆传》(Vita Hieron.) 的作者；他用了将近三十个对开本书页，叙述哲罗姆死后人们对他及其遗物的敬奉。↩︎

  110. 罗马天主教传记作者大多是不加保留地颂扬哲罗姆的人，如马尔蒂亚奈（Martianay）、瓦拉尔西（Vallarsi）、斯蒂尔廷（Stilting）、多尔奇（Dolci），甚至圣公会的凯夫（Cave）也是如此。另参瓦拉尔西版第xi卷，第282—300页所收《古人论哲罗姆及其著作证言选》（Selecta Veterum testimonia de Hieronymo ejusque scriptis）。不过，蒂耶蒙（Tillemont）因倾向詹森主义（Jansenism），对奥古斯丁更为同情，已开始作出较为明达的判断，斯蒂尔廷为此严厉斥责了他。蒙塔朗贝尔（Montalembert，前引书，第i卷，第402页）称赞哲罗姆是个天才，受热忱激励，受悔罪约束，信仰炽烈，知识渊博；但他也顺带提到哲罗姆“近乎野蛮的暴烈性情”，以及“那无穷无尽、有时流于夸张和矫饰的激烈言辞”。约翰·H. 纽曼博士（Dr. John H. Newman）在尚未从蒲西主义（Puseyism）转向罗马天主教时所发表的意见，显出了道德感在这里与罗马教会权威之间的冲突：“我直言不讳地说，倘若他不是圣徒，他的著作和见解中有些东西会使我退避；但既然情形如此，我反而不敢使自己去反对那近乎大公教会（？）世界对他圣德完美所作的判决。”（《教父时代的教会》〔Church of the Fathers〕，第263页，转引自罗伯逊〔Robertson〕。）路德（Luther）在这里也大胆冲破传统，却忘记了《武加大译本》（Vulgate）甚至对他自己的德文圣经译本也有极大的价值，走到了不公正贬抑的另一极端。他多次明确表示厌恶这位教父，指责他根本不懂得怎样论述基督，只会谈禁食、守贞和无用的修士操练。勒克莱尔（Le Clerc）在《哲罗姆问题研究》（Quaestiones Hieronymianae，Amstel.，1700年，500余页）中，以深厚的学力揭露了他的缺点，却失之公允。莫斯海姆（Mosheim）和施勒克（Schröckh）的态度较为温和，但后者认为，哲罗姆对基督教究竟是功大于过，还是过大于功，尚属疑问。后来的新教历史学家对他的评价变得稍为有利，不过这主要是就他的学识而言，而非他的道德品格；他的书信和论战著作暴露了太多无可置疑的弱点。↩︎

  111. 她的父亲声称自己的家系可追溯至阿伽门农（Agamemnon），她的丈夫则声称自己的家系可追溯至埃涅阿斯（Aeneas）。↩︎

  112. 这种不讲清洁的习惯，是古代苦修圣洁生活不可分离的伴侣；在修士身上已经够糟，在修女身上就更令人难以容忍，甚至作呕。↩︎

  113. 哲罗姆说：“Nesciebat se matrem, ut Christi probaret ancillam.”（“她忘记自己是母亲，为要证明自己是基督的婢女。”）这揭示了修道式圣洁与上帝所命定的自然美德之间的冲突。蒙塔朗贝尔也引用了这段令人反感的话，而且显然表示赞同。↩︎

  114. 哲罗姆说，尤斯托基乌姆（Eustochium）希望偿还母亲的债务——大概是靠别人的帮助。富勒（Fuller）说得公允：“慷慨应当像河流一样，不但有流水，也须有堤岸。”↩︎

  115. 拉丁文作 Sublaqueum 或 Sublacum，位于教皇国境内、罗马以东三十多英里处（巴特勒〔Butler〕说“近四十英里”，蒙塔朗贝尔，第ii卷，第7页，则说“五十英里”），在特韦罗内河（Teverone）畔。巴特勒将此地形容为“一片荒凉可怖的连绵岩山，谷中有一条河和一个湖”。↩︎

  116. Monasterium Cassinense（卡西诺修道院）。诚然，它早在583年便被伦巴第人（Lombards）摧毁，据说本尼狄克（Benedict）曾预言此事；但它于731年重建，748年祝圣，857年又被撒拉森人（Saracens）摧毁，约950年再度重建，又历经许多灾难，至1649年得到更彻底的重建，1727年由本尼狄克十三世（Benedict XIII）第三次祝圣。它在皇帝和教皇的庇护下日益富裕扩张，但近代却被剥夺了巨额收入（十六世纪末估计为500,000杜卡特），历经沧桑，至今仍然屹立。在其鼎盛时期，院长是那不勒斯王国（Naples）的首席男爵，统辖四百多个城镇和村庄，院内有数百名修士；但1843年仅有二十人。它拥有相当丰富的藏书。蒙塔朗贝尔（前引书，第ii卷，第19页）称卡西诺山（Monte Cassino）为“天主教世界最有权势、最负盛名的修道院；它之所以著名，尤其因为本尼狄克在此撰写了他的会规，塑造了一种范型，供无数服从这部至高法典的团体效法”。他还引述了但丁（Dante）《天堂篇》（Paradiso）中的诗意描写。路易吉·托斯蒂修士（Dom Luigi Tosti）于1842年在那不勒斯出版了这所修道院的完整历史，共三卷。↩︎

  117. 贵格利（Gregor.），《对话录》（Dial.），第ii卷，第37章。↩︎

  118. “Scienter nesciens, et sapienter indoctus.”（“有意识地保持无知，明智地不受学问熏陶。”）↩︎

  119. 巴特勒在前引书中，甚至将他与摩西和以利亚相比。“他蒙上帝拣选，如同另一位摩西，引领忠信的灵魂进入真正的应许之地，就是天国，因此得了卓越的超自然恩赐，甚至包括行神迹和说预言的恩赐。他仿佛另一位以利沙，蒙上帝赋予非凡的能力，能号令整个自然界，又像古代的先知一样预见未来之事。他常常鼓舞修士们低落的勇气，以十字架的记号挫败魔鬼的各种诡计；建造修道院时，用一个简短的祷告便使最沉重的石头变轻；又在众人面前，使一个在卡西诺山被倒塌的墙压死的初学修士复活。”蒙塔朗贝尔省略了那些更为离奇的神迹，只有普拉西杜斯（Placidus）从漩涡中获救一事例外；他采用博叙埃（Bossuet）的措辞叙述此事，见第ii卷，第15页。↩︎

  120. 天主教会除圣本尼狄克会规外，还认可另外三种会规，即：1. 圣巴西尔（St. Basil）的会规，东方修士至今仍遵守；2. 圣奥古斯丁的会规，为律修司铎、宣道兄弟会即多明我会（Dominicans），以及几个军事修会所采用；3. 十三世纪阿西西的圣方济各（St. Francis of Assisi）及其托钵修会的会规。↩︎

  121. 教皇贵格利甚至相信圣本尼狄克会规是直接受默示而成的；博叙埃（Panégyric de Saint Benoit，《圣本尼狄克颂》）显然言过其实，称它为“基督教的缩影，是福音一切教义、圣教父一切制度和一切全德劝谕的精深而奥秘的纲要”。蒙塔朗贝尔也以类似的法国式铺张雄辩来谈论它。修道主义对于自由的福音所知甚少，竟把基督教化为一种新的顺服律法。↩︎

  122. 这段序言的节录，我们采用了蒙塔朗贝尔的译文，见第ii卷，第44页及下页。↩︎

  123. 第5章：“Primus humilitatis gradus est obedientia sine mora. Haec convenit iis, qui nihil sibi Christo carius aliquid existimant; propter servitium sanctum, quod professi sunt, seu propter metum gehennae, vel gloriam vitae aeternae, mox ut aliquid imperatum a majore fuerit, ac si divinitus imperetur, moram pati nesciunt in faciendo.”（“谦卑的第一阶是不迟延地顺服。这适合那些认定没有什么比基督更为宝贵的人；他们因所誓愿承担的神圣服事，或因惧怕地狱，或因向往永生的荣耀，一听见上级的命令，便如同领受上帝的命令一般，毫不迟延地实行。”）↩︎

  124. 第48章：“Otiositas inimica est animae; et ideo certis temporibus occupari debent fratres in labore manuum, certis iterum horis in lectione divina.”（“闲懒是灵魂的仇敌；因此，弟兄们应在规定的时段从事手工劳动，又在另一些规定的时辰诵读圣书。”）↩︎

  125. 法定日课时辰（horaecanonicae）为 Nocturnae vigiliae, Matutinae, Prima、Tertia, Sexta, Nona, Vespera 和 Completorium，即夜祷、晨祷、第一时辰祷、第三时辰祷、第六时辰祷、第九时辰祷、晚祷和寝前祷。这些规定（第16章）取自对《诗篇》一百一十九篇164节“我因你公义的典章一天七次赞美你”，以及62节“我半夜必起来称谢你”的字面解释。《诗篇》是修道院的礼仪书和圣诗集。全书分配在每日七次礼拜中，使整部《诗篇》每周咏唱一遍。↩︎

  126. 第59章：“Si quis forte de nobilibus offert filium suum Deo in monasterio, si ipse puer minori aetate est, parentes ejus faciant petitionem,” 等。（“若有贵族将儿子奉献给上帝，送入修道院，而这孩子尚未成年，则应由其父母提出申请”，等等。）↩︎

  127. 第40章：“Carnium quadrupedum ab omnibus abstinetur comestio, praeter omnino debiles et aegrotos.”（“除极其虚弱者和病人外，所有人都须禁食四足兽的肉。”）甚至禽肉也在禁食之列；马比永（Mabillon）根据同时代的图尔的贵格利（Gregory of Tours）的证言指出，当时禽肉只是王公贵族的珍馐。↩︎

  128. 第66章：“Monasterium, si possit fieri, ita debet construi, ut omnia necessaria, id est, aqua, molendinum, hortus, pistrinum, vel artes diversae intra monasterium exerceantur, ut non sit necessitas monachis vagandi foras, quia omnino non expedit animabus eorum.”（“修道院若有可能，应如此建造，使一切必需之物，即水源、磨坊、园圃、烘焙作坊，以及各种技艺作业，都能在院内具备并进行，免得修士必须外出游荡，因为这对他们的灵魂全然无益。”）↩︎

  129. 这位格朗弗伊修院（Glanfeuil，即 St. Maur sur Loire）的创建者毛鲁斯（Maurus），是本尼狄克会一个支派的主保圣徒；这个支派就是法国著名的圣毛尔会（Maurians，始于1618年）。十七世纪及十八世纪初，该会以精深的考古和历史研究，以及优良的教父著作版本，卓然著称。圣毛尔会最杰出的学者有：D.（Dom，相当于 Domnus，意为先生）梅纳尔（Menard）、达舍里（d’Achery）、戈丹（Godin）、马比永、勒努里（le Nourry）、马尔蒂亚奈、吕伊纳尔（Ruinart）、马尔泰纳（Martene）、蒙福孔（Montfaucon）、马叙埃（Massuet）、加尼耶（Garnier）和德拉吕（de la Rue）；以及我们时代索莱姆修道院（Solesme）的皮特拉修士（Dom Pitra），他编纂了一部珍贵的教父残篇集。↩︎

  130. 他是最后一位罗马执政官——这一官职被查士丁尼（Justinian）废除——并先后担任奥多亚塞（Odoacer）、狄奥多里克（Theodoric）和阿塔拉里克（Athalaric）的大臣，最后这位君主任命他为禁卫大区长官。↩︎

  134. 《驳约维尼安》（Adv. Jovin.），第i卷，第40章（《著作集》〔Opera〕，第ii卷，第304页）：“Et tamen iste formosus monachus, crassus, nitidus, dealbatus, et quasi sponsus semper incedens, aut uxorem ducat ut aequalem virginitatem nuptiis probet; aut, si non duxerit, frustra contra nos verbis agit, cum opere nobiscum sit.”（“然而，这个漂亮的修士，肥胖、光鲜、白白净净，走起路来总像个新郎；他要么娶妻，以证明守贞与婚姻平等；要么，若不娶妻，他用言辞反对我们也是徒劳，因为他在行为上却与我们站在一起。”）↩︎

  135. 奥古斯丁在《论异端》（De haer.）第82章说：“Cito ista haeresis oppressa et extincta est;”（“这个异端很快就被压制而消灭了。”）哲罗姆则在406年所写《驳维吉兰提乌斯》（Adv. Vigilant.）第1章中说到约维尼安（Jovinian）：他被罗马教会的权威定罪之后，便在纵欲享乐中耗散了心智。↩︎

  137. 《提摩太前书》三章2、12节。↩︎

  138. 《提摩太前书》五章14节；参《提摩太前书》二章15节；《希伯来书》十三章4节。↩︎

  139. 例如，他根据《哥林多前书》七章1节（“男不近女倒好”），毫不加以限定地推论说，见第i卷，第7章（《著作集》〔Opera〕，第ii卷，第246页）：“Si bonum est mulierem non tangere, malum est ergo tangere, nihil enim bono contrarium est, nisi malum; si autem malum est, et ignoscitur, ideo conceditur, ne malo quid deterius fiat .... Tolle fornicationem, et non dicet [apostolus], unusquisque uxorem suam habeat.”（“若不近女是好的，那么近女就是恶的，因为与善相反的只有恶；若这是恶，却又得到宽恕，那么准许此事，是为了免得发生比这恶更坏的事……除去淫乱，〔使徒〕就不会说：男子当各有自己的妻子。”）紧接着（第ii卷，第247页），他又根据保罗在《帖撒罗尼迦前书》五章17节中“不住地祷告”的劝勉推论说：“Si semper orandum est, nunquam ergo conjugio serviendum, quoniam quotiescunque uxori debitum reddo, orare non possum.”（“若必须时常祷告，就绝不该履行夫妻之事，因为每当我向妻子尽本分时，我就不能祷告。”）这类诡辩和曲解，显然出于对婚姻最卑下的肉欲观念，甚至在那个时代也引起了一些反对。他自己后来也觉得走得太远，便在致帕马基乌斯（Pammachium）的第48封信（瓦拉尔西版〔ed. Vallars.〕；本尼狄克会版〔ed. Bened.〕为第30封）中，试图借区分辩论操练式的写法（论战性的修辞）与教义式的写法，来为自己解围。↩︎

  140. 《论婚姻之善》（De bono conj.），第8章：“Duo bona sunt connubium et continentia, quorum alterum est melius.”（“婚姻和节欲都是善的，其中一个更好。”）↩︎

  141. 《哥林多后书》九章6节。↩︎

  142. 他在反驳此人的著作一开头，就称此人为“hominem rusticum et vix primis quoque imbutum literis”（“一个粗鄙之人，连最初步的文字教育都几乎没有受过”）。↩︎

  143. 参《马太福音》二十八章20节。↩︎

  144. 例如，宗教改革者中的路德（他甚至称赫尔维迪乌斯〔Helvidius〕为“十足的傻瓜”）和慈运理（Zuingle）；以及后来的神学家中的奥尔斯豪森（Olshausen）和 J. P. 兰格（J. P. Lange）。↩︎

  145. 关于他的出身，参瓦尔希（Walch）前引书，第675—677页的冗长论述；此人虽令人厌倦，考证却很扎实。↩︎

  146. 他在致里帕里乌斯（Riparius）的第109封信（Epist. 109, ad Ripar.，《著作集》〔Opera〕，第i卷，第719页）中重复了这个浅薄的双关语，说 Vigilantius（警醒者）这一名字是 κατ’ ἀντίφρασιν*，其实倒应该叫 Dormitantius（昏睡者）。事实上，维吉兰提乌斯对于当时某些迷信确实有十分清醒的认识。↩︎

  147. 在高卢南部，即今加斯科涅（Gascogne）的卡塞尔（Casères）。由于经营旅店与圣职不相容，故有人认为，所谓 caupo Calagurritanus（卡拉古里斯的旅店主人）乃是维吉兰提乌斯的父亲。参勒斯勒（Rössler）《教父文库》（Bibliothek der Kirchenväter），第ix部分，第880页及下页，注100；以及瓦尔希前引书。↩︎

  148. 《驳维吉兰提乌斯》（Adv. Vigil.），第1、2章（《著作集》〔Opera〕，第ii卷，第387页及后）。↩︎

  149. “Cinerarios et idolatras, qui mortuorum ossa venerantur.”（“那些崇敬死者骨骸的骨灰崇拜者和偶像崇拜者。”）哲罗姆（Hieron.），致里帕里乌斯的第109封信（Ep. 109, ad Riparium），第i卷，第719页。↩︎

  150. 埃皮法尼乌斯（Epiph.），《驳异端》（Haer.），第75篇。另参瓦尔希（Walch）前引书，第iii卷，第321–338页。贝拉明（Bellarmine）因这一表面上的相似之处，将新教称为埃里乌斯派（Aerian）异端。↩︎
